data-component="copy-link"><span class="m_8d3afb97 mantine-ActionIcon-icon"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg"><path d="M3.9 12c0-1.71 1.39-3.1 3.1-3.1h4V7H7c-2.76 0-5 2.24-5 5s2.24 5 5 data-mantine-styles="inline">.__m__-_R_2muqssm_{--col-flex-grow:auto;--col-flex-basis:100%;--col-max-width:100%;}@media(min-width: var(--grid-column-gap));}}</style><div class="m_96bdd299 mantine-Grid-col __m__-_R_2muqssm_"><article class="blog-article-body"><address class="article-author visually-hidden">By <span><a rel="author" href="/blog/authors/luigino-camastra">Luigino Camastra</a></span><span>, <a rel="author" href="/blog/authors/igor-morgenstern">Igor Morgenstern</a></span><span>, <a rel="author" href="/blog/authors/jan-holman">Jan Holman</a></span></address><div style="--container-size:var(--container-size-xxl);max-width:calc(97.5rem * var(--mantine-scale));padding-inline:0rem" class="m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-size="xxl" data-strategy="block" id="post" data-component="post-content"><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Introduction</strong></p><p>We recently discovered an <code>APT</code> campaign we are calling <code>Operation Dragon Castling</code>. The campaign is targeting what appears to be betting companies in <code>South East Asia</code>, more specifically companies located in <code>Taiwan</code>, the <code>Philippines</code>, and <code>Hong Kong</code>. With moderate confidence, we can attribute the campaign to a <code>Chinese speaking APT group</code>, but unfortunately cannot attribute the attack to a specific group and are not sure what the attackers are after.</p><p>We found notable code similarity between one of the modules used by this APT group (the <code>MulCom backdoor</code>) and the <code>FFRat</code> samples described by the <code>BlackBerry Cylance Threat Research Team</code> in their <code>2017</code> <a href="https://blogs.blackberry.com/en/2017/06/breaking-down-ff-rat-malware">report</a> and <code>Palo Alto Networks</code> in their <code>2015</code> <a href="https://researchcenter.paloaltonetworks.com/2015/04/unit-42-identifies-new-dragonok-backdoor-malware-deployed-against-japanese-targets/">report</a>. Based on this, we suspect that the FFRat codebase is being shared between several Chinese adversary groups. Unfortunately, this is not sufficient for attribution as FFRat itself was never reliably attributed.</p><p>In this blogpost we will describe the malware used in these attacks and the backdoor planted by the APT group, as well as other malicious files used to gain persistence and access to the infected machines. We will also discuss the two infection vectors we saw being used to deliver the malware: an infected installer and exploitation of a vulnerable legitimate application, <code>WPS</code> <code>Office</code>.</p><p>We identified a new vulnerability (<a href="https://cve.mitre.org/cgi-bin/cvename.cgi?name=CVE-2022-24934">CVE-2022-24934</a>) in the WPS Office updater wpsupdate.exe, which we suspect that the attackers abused.</p><p>We would like to thank Taiwan’s <a href="https://teamt5.org/en/"><code>TeamT5</code></a> for providing us with IoCs related to the infection vector.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/final.drawio.png.webp?itok=xtgtPzOl 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/final.drawio.png.webp?itok=yFwwuMuO 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/final.drawio.png.webp?itok=R6dvwU2H 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/final.drawio.png.webp?itok=PbYqrQoO 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Ffinal.drawio.png.webp%3Fitok%3Dz8ERjtcT&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Ffinal.drawio.png.webp%3Fitok%3Dz8ERjtcT&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>In the diagram above, we describe the relations between the malicious files. Some of the relations might not be accurate, e.g. we are not entirely sure if the MulCom backdoor is loaded by the <code>CorePlugin</code>. However, we strongly believe that it is one of the malicious files used in this campaign.&nbsp;</p><h2><strong>Infection Vector</strong></h2><p>We’ve seen multiple infection vectors used in this campaign. Among others, an attacker sent an email with an infected installer to the support team of one of the targeted companies asking to check for a bug in their software. In this post, we are going to describe another vector we’ve seen: a fake <code>WPS Office</code> update package. We suspect an attacker exploited a bug in the WPS updater<code> wpsupdate.exe</code>, which is a part of the WPS Office installation package. We have contacted WPS Office team about the vulnerability (<a href="https://cve.mitre.org/cgi-bin/cvename.cgi?name=CVE-2022-24934"><code>CVE-2022-24934</code></a>), which we discovered, and it has since been fixed.</p><p>During our investigation we saw suspicious behavior in the WPS updater process. When analyzing the binary we discovered a potential security issue that allows an attacker to use the updater to communicate with a server controlled by the attacker to perform actions on the victim’s system, including downloading and running arbitrary executables. To exploit the vulnerability, a registry key under <code>HKEY_CURRENT_USER </code>needs to be modified, and by doing this an attacker gains persistence on the system and control over the update process. In the case we analyzed, the malicious binary was downloaded from the domain <code>update.wps[.]cn</code>, which is a domain belonging to <code>Kingsoft</code>, but the serving IP (<code>103.140.187.16</code>) has no relationship to the company, so we assume that it is a fake update server used by the attackers.&nbsp;<br>The downloaded binary (<code>setup_CN_2052_11.1.0.8830_PersonalDownload_Triale.exe - B9BEA7D1822D9996E0F04CB5BF5103C48828C5121B82E3EB9860E7C4577E2954</code>) drops two files for sideloading: a signed <code>QMSpeedupRocketTrayInjectHelper64.exe - Tencent Technology (a3f3bc958107258b3aa6e9e959377dfa607534cc6a426ee8ae193b463483c341)</code> and a malicious DLL<code> QMSpeedupRocketTrayStub64.dll.</code></p><h2><strong>Dropper 1 (QMSpeedupRocketTrayStub64.dll)</strong></h2><p>76adf4fd93b70c4dece4b536b4fae76793d9aa7d8d6ee1750c1ad1f0ffa75491</p><p>The first stage is a backdoor communicating with a C&amp;C (<code>mirrors.centos.8788912[.]com</code>). Before contacting the C&amp;C server, the backdoor performs several preparational operations. It hooks three functions: <code>GetProcAddress</code>, <code>FreeLibrary</code>, <code>LdrUnloadDll</code>.&nbsp;To get the C&amp;C domain, it maps itself to the memory and reads data starting at the offset <code>1064 </code>from the end. The domain name is not encrypted in any way and is stored as a wide string in clear text in the binary.&nbsp;</p><p>Then it initializes an object for a <code>JScript</code> class with the named item <code>ScriptHelper</code>.&nbsp; The dropper uses the <code>ImpersonateLoggedOnUser </code>API Call to re-use a token from <code>explorer.exe</code> so it effectively runs under the same user. Additionally, it uses <code>RegOverridePredefKey </code>to redirect the current <code>HKEY_CURRENT_USER </code>to <code>HKEY_CURRENT_USER&nbsp; </code>of an impersonated user.&nbsp;For communication with C&amp;C it constructs a UserAgent string with some system information e.g. <code>Mozilla/4.0 (compatible; MSIE 9.0; Windows NT 6.1;.NET CLR 2.0). The information that is exfiltrated is: Internet Explorer version, Windows version, the value of the “<em>User Agent\Post Platform”</em></code><em> </em>registry values.</p><p>After that, the sample constructs <code>JScript </code>code to execute. The header of the code contains definitions of two variables: <code>server</code> with the C&amp;C domain name and a hardcoded <code>key</code>. Then it sends the HTTP <code>GET </code>request to <code>/api/connect,</code> the response should be encrypted <code>JScript </code>code that is decrypted, appended to the constructed header and executed using the <code>JScript </code>class created previously.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>At the time of analysis, the C&amp;C was not responding, but from the telemetry data we can conclude that it was downloading the next stage from <code>hxxp://mirrors.centos.8788912.com/upload/ea76ad28a3916f52a748a4f475700987.exe</code> to <code>%ProgramData%\icbc_logtmp.exe</code> and executing it.</p><h2><strong>Dropper 2 (IcbcLog)</strong></h2><p>a428351dcb235b16dc5190c108e6734b09c3b7be93c0ef3d838cf91641b328b3</p><p>The second dropper is a runner that, when executed, tries to escalate privileges via the <code>COM Session Moniker Privilege </code>Escalation <code>(</code><a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/security-updates/securitybulletins/2017/ms17-012"><code>MS17-012</code></a><code>)</code>, then dropping a few binaries, which are stored with the following resource IDs:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-27%20at%209.11.05%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=4J9bzYuO 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-27%20at%209.11.05%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=Wez5nehr 2x" media="(min-width: srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-27%20at%209.11.05%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=wV_3xg6z 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-27%20at%209.11.05%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=gFyHHPxM 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img alt="" width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" style="color:transparent;width:100%;height:100%" srcSet="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-27%2520at%25209.11.05%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3DbqxfmsKU&amp;w=384&amp;q=75 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-27%2520at%25209.11.05%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3DbqxfmsKU&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-27%2520at%25209.11.05%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3DbqxfmsKU&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The encrypted payloads have the following structure:</p><p>The encryption key is a wide string starting from offset <code>0x8</code>. The encrypted data starts at the offset <code>0x528</code>. To decrypt the data, a SHA256 hash of the key is created using <code>CryptHashData </code>API, and is then used with a hard-coded IV<code> 0123456789abcde</code> to decrypt the data using <code>CryptDecrypt </code>API with the <code>AES256</code> algorithm. After that, the decrypted data is decompressed with <code>RtlDecompressBuffer</code>. To verify that the decryption went well, the <code>CRC32</code> of the data is computed and compared to the value at the offset <code>0x4 </code>of the original resource data. When all the payloads are dropped to the disk, <code>bdservicehost.exe </code>is executed to run the next stage.</p><h2><strong>Loader (CoreX)</strong></h2><p>97c392ca71d11de76b69d8bf6caf06fa3802d0157257764a0e3d6f0159436c42</p><p>The <code>Loader (CoreX) </code>DLL is sideloaded during the previous stage <code>(Dropper 2)</code> and acts as a dropper. Similarly to <code>Dropper 1</code>, it hooks the <code>GetProcAddress </code>and <code>FreeLibrary </code>API functions. These hooks execute the main code of this library. The main code first checks whether it was loaded by <code>regsvr32.exe</code> and then it retrieves encrypted data from its resources. This data is dropped into the same folder as <code>syscfg.dat</code>. The file is then loaded and decrypted using AES-256 with the following options for setup:</p><ul><li data-list-item-id="efbbd9daa6ff2673271f3fa7e2e9c70c3">Key is the computer name and IV is <code>qwertyui12345678</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e276c68eaeda9eaa8e7c55fe40bd85bef">AES-256 setup parameters are embedded in the resource in the format <code>&lt;key&gt;#&lt;IV&gt;</code>. So you may e.g. see <code>cbfc2vyuzckloknf#8o3yfn0uee429m8d</code></li></ul></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/hex.png.webp?itok=7IaymrJj 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/hex.png.webp?itok=bunxx5md 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/hex.png.webp?itok=nkdVNpiP 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/hex.png.webp?itok=LdUH3tWJ 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/hex.png.webp?itok=Uj6Nz-LI 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/hex.png.webp?itok=8DaRP31S 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img alt="AES-256 setup parameters" width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" style="color:transparent;width:100%;height:100%" srcSet="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fhex.png.webp%3Fitok%3DUj6Nz-LI&amp;w=384&amp;q=75 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fhex.png.webp%3Fitok%3DUj6Nz-LI&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fhex.png.webp%3Fitok%3DUj6Nz-LI&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><p class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">AES-256 setup parameters</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The main code continues to check if the process <code>ekrn.exe</code> is running. <code>ekrn.exe</code> is an ESET Kernel service. If the ESET Kernel service is running, it will try to remap <code>ntdll.dll</code>. We assume that this is used to bypass <code>ntdll.dll</code> hooking.&nbsp;</p><p>After a service check, it will decompress and execute shellcode, which in turn loads a DLL with the next stage. The DLL is stored, unencrypted, as part of the shellcode.&nbsp;The shellcode enumerates exports of <code>ntdll.dll </code>and builds an array with hashes of names of all <code>Zw*</code> functions (windows native API system calls) then sorts them by their RVA. By doing this, the shellcode exploits the fact that the order of RVAs of <code>Zw*</code> functions equals the order of the corresponding syscalls, so an index of the <code>Zw*</code> function in this array is a syscall number, which can be called using the syscall instruction. Security solutions can therefore be bypassed based on the hooking of the API in userspace. Finally, the embedded core module DLL is loaded and executed.</p><h2><strong>Proto8 (Core module)</strong></h2><p>f3ed09ee3fe869e76f34eee1ef974d1b24297a13a58ebff20ea4541b9a2d86c7</p><p>The core module is a single DLL that is responsible for setting up the malware’s working directory, loading configuration files, updating its code, loading plugins, beaconing to C&amp;C servers and waiting for commands.</p><p>It has a cascading structure with four steps:</p><h3><strong>Step 1</strong></h3><p>The first part is dedicated to initial checks and a few evasion techniques.&nbsp;At first, the core module verifies that the DLL is being run by <code>spdlogd.exe</code> (an executable used for persistence, see below) or that it is not being run by <code>rundll32.exe.</code> If this check fails, the execution terminates. The DLL proceeds by hooking the <code>GetProcAddress </code>and <code>FreeLibrary </code>functions in order to execute the main function, similarly to the previous infection stages.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/in-googo.png.webp?itok=2TTA-56h class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The malware then creates a new window (named <code>Sample</code>) with a custom callback function. A message with the ID <code>0x411 </code>is sent to the window via <code>SendMessageW</code> which causes the aforementioned callback to execute the main function. The callback function can also process the <code>0x412 </code>message ID, even though no specific functionality is tied to it.</p></div><div class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">The window callback only contains implementation for message 0x411 but there is a check for 0x412 as well</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h3><strong>Step 2</strong></h3><p>In the second step, the module tries to self-update, load configuration files and set up its working directory (WD).</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/step-2-dark-w-logo.png.webp?itok=GjwZnzl- 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/step-2-dark-w-logo.png.webp?itok=cvnHGE8F 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source 2x" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h4><strong>Self-update</strong></h4><p>The malware first looks for a file called <code>new_version.dat</code> – if it exists, its content is loaded into memory, executed in a new thread and a debug string <code>“run code ok”</code> is printed out. We did not come across this file, but based on its name and context, this is most likely a self update functionality.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/run-code-ok.png.webp?itok=htarqsb- 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/run-code-ok.png.webp?itok=tNyX60xH 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/run-code-ok.png.webp?itok=rzOWTO5X 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/run-code-ok.png.webp?itok=k4c-0zTd 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/run-code-ok.png.webp?itok=1mGImiZm 1x, height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" style="color:transparent;width:100%;height:100%" srcSet="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frun-code-ok.png.webp%3Fitok%3D1mGImiZm&amp;w=384&amp;q=75 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frun-code-ok.png.webp%3Fitok%3D1mGImiZm&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frun-code-ok.png.webp%3Fitok%3D1mGImiZm&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Load configuration file <code>inst.dat </code>and set up working directory. First, the core module configuration file inst.dat is searched for in the following three locations:</p><ul><li data-list-item-id="ea830906ebfe7209f2529134f5e37ca4d">the directory where the core module DLL is located</li><li data-list-item-id="ec3ea1b97a307a994062abf67820446f3">the directory where the EXE that loaded the core module DLL it is located</li><li data-list-item-id="eb3eb29147f89b253ca92488c246b260b"><code>C:\ProgramData\</code></li></ul><p>It contains the path to the malware’s working directory in plaintext. If it is not found, a hard-coded directory name is used and the directory is created. The working directory is a location the malware uses to drop or read any files it uses in subsequent execution phases.</p><p>Load configuration file <code>smcache.dat</code>.</p><p>After the working directory is set up, the sample will load the configuration file <code>smcache.dat</code> from it. This file contains the domains, protocols and port numbers used to communicate with C&amp;C servers (details in Step 4) plus a <code>“comment”</code> string. This string is likely used to identify the campaign or individual victims. It is used to create an empty file on the victim’s computer (see below) and it’s also sent as a part of the initial beacon when communicating with C&amp;C servers. We refer to it as the<code> “comment string”</code> because we have seen a few versions of smcache.dat where the content of the string was<code> “the comment string here”</code> and it is also present in another configuration file with the name <code>comment.dat</code> which has the INI file format and contains this string under the key COMMENT.</p><p>Create a <code>log </code>file</p><p>Right after the sample finds and reads smcache.dat, it creates a file based on the victim’s username and the comment string from smcache.dat. If the comment string is not present, it will use a default hard-coded value (for example <code>M86_99.lck</code>). Based on the extension it could be a log of some sort, but we haven’t seen any part of the malware writing into it so it could just serve as a lockfile. After the file is successfully created, the malware creates a mutex and goes on to the next step.</p><h3><strong>Step 3</strong></h3><p>Next, the malware collects information about the infected environment (such as username, DNS and NetBios computer names as well as OS version and architecture) and sets up its internal structures, most notably a list of <code>“call objects”</code>. Call objects are structures each associated with a particular function and saved into a <code>“dispatcher”</code> structure in a map with hard-coded 4-byte keys. These keys are later used to call the functions based on commands from C&amp;C servers.&nbsp;</p><p>The key values (IDs) seem to be structured, where the first three bytes are always the same within a given sample, while the last byte is always the same for a given usage across all the core module samples that we’ve seen. For example, the function that calls the <code>RevertToSelf </code>function is identified by the number <code>0x20210326 </code>in some versions of the core module that we’ve seen and<code> <em>0x19181726</em> </code>in others. This suggests that the first three bytes of the ID number are tied to the core module version, or more likely the infrastructure version, while the last byte is the actual ID of a function.&nbsp;</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-27%2520at%25209.13.40%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3DkqQtQfgJ&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-27%2520at%25209.13.40%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3DkqQtQfgJ&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Webdav&nbsp;</strong></p><p>While initializing the call objects the core module also tries to connect to the URL <code>hxxps://dav.jianguoyun.com/dav/</code> with the username <code>12121jhksdf </code>and password <code>121121212 </code>by calling <code>WNetAddConnection3W</code>. This address was not responsive at the time of analysis but <code>jianguoyun[.]com</code> is a Chinese file sharing service. Our hypothesis is that this is either a way to get plugin code or an updated version of the core module itself.</p><p><strong>Plugins</strong></p><p>The core module contains a function that receives a buffer with plugin DLL data, saves it into a file with the name <code>kbg&lt;tick_count&gt;.dat</code> in the malware working directory, loads it into memory and then calls its exported function <code>InitCorePlug</code>. The plugin file on disk is set to be deleted on reboot by calling <code>MoveFileExW </code>with the parameter <code>MOVEFILE_DELAY_UNTIL_REBOOT</code>.&nbsp;For more information about the plugins, see the dedicated Plugins section.</p><h3><strong>Step 4</strong></h3><p>In the final step, the malware will iterate over C&amp;C servers contained in the smcache.dat configuration file and will try to reach each one.&nbsp;The structure of the <code>smcache.dat</code> config file is as follows:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/struct.png.webp?itok=4oVMguxd 1x, 2x" class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">The structure of the smcache.dat config file</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The protocol string can have one of nine possible values:&nbsp;</p><ul><li data-list-item-id="e071b461312ed0c75d97d52412ea7fc1d"><code>TCP</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e02d88afe539aa83d2f17dd7b0f446d16"><code>HTTPS</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e5a6f5dc95319b788e55b5375197116a1"><code>UDP</code></li><li data-list-item-id="ec6f513e4a2a9eaf13c5588b84315d8a3"><code>DNS</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e39740f5f847718ce4630c30ea436921a"><code>ICMP</code></li><li data-list-item-id="eca0e286a0ac0dbc07b5885167396adf6"><code>HTTPSIPV6</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e06fea103c13d7af71b29a280c45ba276"><code>WEB</code></li><li data-list-item-id="eceb2b0c0d488753703b30eef032558a3"><code>SSH</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e2799357f897faba65dfdf6c55b717b79"><code>HTTP</code></li></ul><p>Depending on the protocol tied to the particular C&amp;C domain, the malware sets up the connection, sends a beacon to the C&amp;C and waits for commands.</p><p>In this blogpost, we will mainly focus on the HTTP protocol option as we’ve seen it being used by the attackers.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>When using the HTTP protocol, the core module first opens two persistent request handles – one for <code>POST </code>and one for <code>GET </code>requests, both to <code>“/connect”</code>. These handles are tested by sending an empty buffer in the <code>POST </code>request and checking the HTTP status code of the <code>GET </code>request. Following this, the malware sends the initial beacon to the C&amp;C server by calling the <code>InternetWriteFile </code>API with the previously opened <code>POST </code>request handle and reads data from the <code>GET </code>request handle by calling <code>InternetReadFile</code>.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/wireshark-2-1536x537.png.webp?itok=xl9cXemc 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/wireshark-2-1536x537.png.webp?itok=cwOs2viZ 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/wireshark-2-1536x537.png.webp?itok=_VNyXJ0U 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/wireshark-2-1536x537.png.webp?itok=ebp36y9E 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img alt="HTTP POST beacon" width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" style="color:transparent;width:100%;height:100%" srcSet="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fwireshark-2-1536x537.png.webp%3Fitok%3D_VNyXJ0U&amp;w=384&amp;q=75 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fwireshark-2-1536x537.png.webp%3Fitok%3D_VNyXJ0U&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fwireshark-2-1536x537.png.webp%3Fitok%3D_VNyXJ0U&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><p class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">HTTP POST beacon</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The core module uses the following (mostly hard-coded) HTTP headers:</p><ul><li data-list-item-id="e681e659a23296adb0eeb41801c3d4544"><code>Accept: */*</code></li><li data-list-item-id="ebf17a32e099479702b8ee4ef9e8a2542"><code>x-cid: {<em>&lt;uuid&gt;</em>}</code> – new uuid is generated for each <code>GET/POST </code>request pair</li><li data-list-item-id="eaf5b6cfcd07a2f9ac8a3207a52a31ad4"><code>Pragma: no-cache</code></li><li data-list-item-id="ec4a3a9dc442d42fe736590121754b7e3"><code>Cache-control: no-transform</code></li><li data-list-item-id="eb39c31424ec5afacfe87afb430b9a341"><code>User-Agent: &lt;user_agent&gt;</code> – generated from registry or hard-coded (see below)</li><li data-list-item-id="e463fb035dad06e75a0866458ceb6fe92"><code>Host: &lt;host_value&gt;</code> – C&amp;C server domain or the value from hostcfg.dat (see below)</li><li data-list-item-id="e253ed4f05534b8cb2dda7c9d452079a8"><code>Connection: Keep-Alive</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e9e3bc1db8a2308b3628a4b4eb762547c"><code>Content-Length: 4294967295</code> (max uint, only in the POST request)</li></ul><p><strong>User-Agent header</strong></p><p>The User-Agent string is constructed from the registry the same way as in the <code>Dropper 1</code> module (including the logged-on user impersonation when accessing registry) or a hard-coded string is used if the registry access fails: <code>“Mozilla/4.0 (compatible; MSIE 8.0; Windows NT 6.1; WOW64; Trident/4.0; SLCC2; .NET CLR 2.0.50727; .NET CLR 3.5.30729; .NET CLR 3.0.30729; Media Center PC 6.0)”</code>.</p><p><strong>Host header</strong></p><p>When setting up this header, the malware looks for either a resource with the<code> ID 1816</code> or a file called <code>hostcfg.dat</code> if the resource is not found. If the resource or file is found, the content is used as the value in the <code>Host </code>HTTP header for all C&amp;C communication instead of the C&amp;C domain found in <code>smcache.dat</code>. It does not change the actual C&amp;C domain to which the request is made – this suggests the possibility of the C&amp;C server being behind a reverse proxy.</p><p><strong>Initial beacon</strong></p><p>The first data packet the malware sends to a C&amp;C server contains a base64 encoded LZNT1-compressed buffer, including a newly generated uuid (different from the uuid used in the x-cid header), the victim’s username, OS version and architecture, computer DNS and BIOS names and the comment string found in <code>smcache.dat </code>or<code> comment.dat</code>. The value from <code>comment.dat</code> takes precedence if this file exists.<em>&nbsp;</em></p><p>In the core module sample we analyzed, there was actually a typo in the function that reads the value from<code> comment.dat </code>– it looks for the key<code> “COMMNET”</code> instead of <code>“COMMENT”</code>.</p><p>After this, the malware enters a loop waiting for commands from the C&amp;C server in the form of the ID value of one of the call objects.<br>Each message sent to the C&amp;C server contains a hard-coded four byte number value with the same structure as the values used as keys in the call-object map. The ID numbers associated with messages sent to C&amp;C servers that we’ve seen are:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Interesting observations about the protocols, other than the HTTP protocol:</p><ul><li data-list-item-id="e2c14678a1667776f3f4bd49f490c64e9">HTTPS does not use persistent request handles</li><li data-list-item-id="ecab0082326cc8b8ab3783111165c12ee">HTTPS uses HTTP <code>GET </code>request with data Base64-encoded in the cookie header to send the initial beacon</li><li data-list-item-id="edd54c44891fa0a09ee75d186cfcedf90">HTTPS, TCP and UDP use a custom “magic” header: <code>Magic-Code: hhjjdfgh</code></li></ul><h3><strong>General observations on the core module</strong></h3></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/nothing.png.webp?itok=uKvsvEus 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/nothing.png.webp?itok=mPwnjHYD 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/nothing.png.webp?itok=Lrt_ljCn 1x, 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fnothing.png.webp%3Fitok%3DLrt_ljCn&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fnothing.png.webp%3Fitok%3DLrt_ljCn&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The core samples we observed often output debug strings via <code>OutputDebugStringA </code>and <code>OutputDebugStringW </code>or by sending them to the C&amp;C server. Examples of debug strings used by the core module are: its filepath at the beginning of execution, <code>“run code ok”</code> after self-update, <code>“In googo”</code> in the hook of <code>GetProcAddress</code>, <code>“recv bomb”</code> and <code>“sent bomb”</code> in the main C&amp;C communicating function, etc.</p><h4><strong>String obfuscation</strong></h4><p>We came across samples of the core module with only cleartext strings but also samples with certain strings obfuscated by XORing them with a unique (per sample) hard-coded key.&nbsp;</p><p>Even within the samples that contain obfuscated strings, there are many cleartext strings present and there seems to be no logic in deciding which string will be obfuscated and which won’t. For example, most format strings are obfuscated, but important IoCs such as credentials or filenames are not.&nbsp;</p><p>To illustrate this: most strings in the function that retrieves a value from the comment.dat file are obfuscated and the call to <code>GetPrivateProfileStringW </code>is dynamically resolved by the <code>GetProcAddress </code>API, but all the strings in the function that writes into the same config file are in cleartext and there is a direct call to <code>WritePrivateProfileStringW</code>.&nbsp;</p><p>Overall, the core module code is quite robust and contains many failsafes and options for different scenarios (for example, the amount of possible protocols used for C&amp;C communication), however, we probably only saw samples of this malware that are still in active development as there are many functions that are not yet implemented and only serve as placeholders.</p><h3><strong>Plugins</strong></h3><p>In the section below, we will describe the functionality of the plugins used by the <code>Core Module (Proto8)</code> to extend its functionality.&nbsp;</p><p>We are going to describe three plugins with various functionalities, such as:</p><ul><li data-list-item-id="e0451cb10a48a83f43a91f4827bf0485f">Achieving persistence</li><li data-list-item-id="eed5ed24a184eae1dff62bd2df15bb93e">Bypassing UAC</li><li data-list-item-id="e2cf0c74e63cc6ea4a62768e7b0cd89d2">Registering an RPC interface</li><li data-list-item-id="e9e56454b2f0fc87510d2c50f49eeb6ee">Creating a new account</li><li data-list-item-id="e16c321342c05f69c7de7a05913675167">Backdoor capabilities</li></ul><h4><strong>Core Plugin</strong></h4><p>0985D65FA981ABD57A4929D8ECD866FC72CE8C286BA9EB252CA180E280BD8755</p><p>This plugin is a DLL binary loaded by the fileless core module (<code>Proto8</code>) as mentioned above. It extends the malware’s functionality by adding methods for managing additional plugins. These additional plugins&nbsp; export the function <code>"GetPlugin"</code> which the core plugin executes.</p><p>This part uses the same command ID based calling convention as the core module (see above), adding three new methods:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>All plugin binaries used by the core module are stored in the working directory under the name<code> kbg&lt;tick_count&gt;.dat</code>. After the <code>Core Plugin</code> is loaded, it first removes all plugins from the working directory – see the image below.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h4><strong>Zload(Atomx.dll,xps1.dll)</strong></h4><p>2ABC43865E49F8835844D30372697FDA55992E5A6A13808CFEED1C37BA8F7876</p><p>The DLL we call <code>Zload </code>is an example of a plugin loaded by the <code>Core Plugin</code>. It exports four functions: <code>“GetPlugin”</code>, <code>“Install”</code>, <code>“core_zload”</code> and <code>”zload”</code>. The main functionality of this plugin is setting up persistence, creating a backdoor user account, and concealing itself on the infected system. We will focus on the exported functions <code>zload</code>, <code>core_zload</code> and the default <code>DllMain </code>function, as they contain the most interesting functionality.</p><h5><strong>Zload (process starter)</strong></h5><p>This function is fairly simple, its main objective is to execute another binary. It first retrieves the path to the directory where the <code>Zload </code>plugin binary is located <code>(&lt;root_folder&gt;)</code> and creates a new subfolder called<code> "mec" </code>in it. After this it renames and moves three files into it:</p><ul><li data-list-item-id="e2f8cdd18b09b15951f518c25c04d0c58">the <code>Zload </code>plugin binary itself as<code> &lt;root_folder&gt;\mec\logexts.dll</code>,</li><li data-list-item-id="e8a8b42d2c981da0149424bdf6b9bf788"><code>&lt;root_folder&gt;\spdlogd.exe</code> as <code>&lt;root_folder&gt;\mec\spdagent.exe</code> and</li><li data-list-item-id="e4c4409f249b8923f75ca02b792bd19a3"><code>&lt;root_folder&gt;\kb.ini</code> as<code> &lt;root_folder&gt;\mec\kb.ini</code></li></ul><p>After the files are renamed and moved, it creates a new process by executing the binary <code>&lt;root_folder&gt;\mec\spdagent.exe</code> (originally <code>&lt;root_folder&gt;\spdlogd.exe</code>).</p><h5><strong>core_zload (persistence setup)</strong></h5><p>This function is responsible for persistence which it achieves by registering itself into the list of security support providers (SSPs). Windows SSP DLLs are loaded into the <code>Local Security Authority (LSA)</code> process when the system boots. The code of this function is notably similar to the <code>mimikat_ssp/AddSecurityPackage_RawRPC</code> <a href="https://github.com/jas502n/mimikat_ssp/blob/master/AddSecurityPackage_RawRPC.cpp">source code found on github</a>.</p><h5><strong>DllMain (sideloading, setup)</strong></h5><p>The default DllMain function leverages several persistence and evasion techniques. It also allows the attacker to create a backdoor account on the infected system and lower the overall system security.</p><h5><strong>Persistence</strong></h5><p>The plugin first checks if its DLL was loaded either by the processes <code>“lsass.exe”</code> or<code> “spdagent.exe”</code>. If the DLL was loaded by <code>“spdagent.exe”</code>, it will adjust the token privileges of the current process.</p><p>If it was loaded by <code>“lsass.exe”</code>, it will retrieve the path <code>“kb&lt;num&gt;.dll”</code> from the configuration file <code>“kb.ini”</code> and write it under the registry key <code>HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\\SYSTEM\\CurrentControlSet\\Services\\WinSock2\\Parameters</code> <code>AutodialDLL</code>. This ensures persistence, as it causes the DLL <code>“kb&lt;num&gt;.dll”</code> to be loaded each time the Winsock 2 library (<code>ws2_32.dll</code>) is invoked.</p><h5><strong>Evasion</strong></h5><p>To avoid detection, the plugin first checks the list of running processes for <code>“avp.exe”</code> (Kaspersky Antivirus) or <code>“NortonSecurity.exe”</code> and exits if either of them is found. If these processes are not found on the system, it goes on to conceal itself by changing its own process name to <code>“explorer.exe”</code>.</p><p>The plugin also has the capability to bypass the UAC mechanisms and to elevate its process privileges through <code>CMSTP COM</code> interfaces, such as <code>CMSTPLUA {3E5FC7F9-9A51-4367-9063-A120244FBEC7}</code>.</p><h5><strong>Backdoor user account creation</strong></h5><p>Next, the plugin carries out registry manipulation (details can be found in the appendix), that lowers the system’s protection by:</p><ul><li data-list-item-id="ef328ae4fb5ae63758f49d787e18b1c51">Allowing local accounts to have full admin rights when they are authenticating via network logon</li><li data-list-item-id="e4fb35498d896510150ab3e69f77a3238">Enabling RDP connections to the machine without the user password</li><li data-list-item-id="e42ad0214d78e736cea0a62b2896826d5">Disabling admin approval on an administrator account, which means that all applications run with full administrative privileges</li><li data-list-item-id="e4de60df007fb064cb97bd9a6df69669d">Enabling anonymous SID to be part of the everyone group in Windows</li><li data-list-item-id="e4e68adfb5d01572c994fec30a7d2d2bb">Allowing <code>“Null Session”</code> users to list users and groups in the domain</li><li data-list-item-id="e9f7ccc77168a7107378d883044c2fe09">Allowing <code>“Null Session”</code> users to access shared folders</li><li data-list-item-id="e4cbbb37529f2345730dcadb89a55e2f1">Setting the name of the pipe that will be accessible to “Null Session” users</li></ul><p>After this step, the plugin changes the <code>WebClient </code>service startup type to <code>“Automatic”</code>. It creates a new user with the name <code>“DefaultAccount”</code> and the password <code>“Admin@1999!”</code> which is then added to the <code>“Administrator”</code> and <code>“Remote Desktop Users”</code> groups. It also hides the new account on the logon screen.</p><p>As the last step, the plugin checks the list of running processes for process names <code>“360tray.exe”</code> and <code>“360sd.exe”</code> and executes the file <code>"spdlogd.exe"</code> if neither of them is found.</p><h4><strong>MecGame(kb%num%.dll)</strong></h4><p>4C73A62A9F19EEBB4FEFF4FDB88E4682EF852E37FFF957C9E1CFF27C5E5D47AD</p><p>MecGame is another example of a plugin that can be loaded by the <code>Core Plugin</code>. Its main purpose is similar to the previously described <code>Zload</code> plugin – it executes the binary <code>“spdlogd.exe”</code> and achieves persistence by registering an RPC interface with <code>UUID {1052E375-2CE2-458E-AA80-F3B7D6EA23AF}</code>. This RPC interface represents a function that decodes and executes a base64 encoded shellcode.</p><p>The <code>MecGame </code>plugin has several methods for executing spdlogd.exe depending on the level of available privileges. It also creates a lockfile with the name <code>MSSYS.lck</code> or <code>&lt;UserName&gt;-XPS.lck</code> depending on the name of the process that loaded it, and deletes the files <code>atomxd.dll </code>and<code> logexts.dll</code>.</p><p>It can be installed as a service with the service name <code>“inteloem”</code> or can be loaded by any executable that connects to the internet via the <code>Winsock2</code> library.</p><h4><strong>MulCom</strong></h4><p>ABA89668C6E9681671A95B3D7A08AAE2A067DEED2D835BA6F6FD18556C88A5F2</p><p>This DLL is a backdoor module which exports four functions: <code>“OperateRoutineW”</code>, <code>“StartRoutineW”</code>, <code>“StopRoutineW”</code> and <code>”WorkRoutineW”</code>; the main malicious function being <code>“StartRoutineW”</code>.</p><p>For proper execution, the backdoor needs configuration data accessed through a shared object with the file mapping name either <code>“Global\\4ED8FD41-2D1B-4CC3-B874-02F0C60FF9CB”</code> or <code>"Local\\4ED8FD41-2D1B-4CC3-B874-02F0C60FF9CB”</code>. Unfortunately we didn’t come across the configuration data, so we are missing some information such as the C&amp;C server domains this module uses.</p><p>There are 15 commands supported by this backdoor (although some of them are not implemented) referred to by the following numerical identifiers:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h5><strong>Communication protocol</strong></h5><p>The <code>MulCom </code>backdoor is capable of communicating via HTTP and TCP protocols. The data it exchanges with the C&amp;C servers is encrypted and compressed by the RC4 and aPack algorithms respectively, using the RC4 key loaded from the configuration data object.</p><p>It is also capable of proxy server authentication using schemes such as Basic, NTLM, Negotiate or to authenticate via either the SOCKS4 and SOCKS5 protocols.</p><p>After successful authentication with a proxy server, the backdoor sends data xorred by the constant <code>0xBC</code>. This data is a set with the following structure:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fgetacp.png.webp%3Fitok%3DaPqMJE2Q&amp;w=384&amp;q=75 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fgetacp.png.webp%3Fitok%3DaPqMJE2Q&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fgetacp.png.webp%3Fitok%3DaPqMJE2Q&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><p class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Data structure</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Another interesting capability of this backdoor is the usage of layered C&amp;C servers. If this option is enabled in the configuration object (it is not the default option), the first request goes to the first layer C&amp;C server, which returns the IP address of the second layer. Any subsequent communication goes to the second layer directly.</p><p>As previously stated, we found several code similarities between the <code>MulCom </code>DLL and the <code>FFRat </code>(a.k.a. <code>FormerFirstRAT</code>).</p><p><strong>Conclusion</strong></p><p>We have described a robust and modular toolset used most likely by a Chinese speaking APT group targeting gambling-related companies in South East Asia. As we mentioned in this blogpost, there are notable code similarities between <code>FFRat </code>samples and the <code>MulCom </code>backdoor. <code>FFRat </code>or <code>"FormerFirstRAT''</code> has been publicly associated with the<code> DragonOK group</code> according to the Palo Alto Network report, which has in turn been associated with backdoors like <code>PoisonIvy</code> and <code>PlugX </code>– tools commonly used by Chinese speaking attackers.</p><p>We also described two different infection vectors, one of which weaponized a vulnerable WPS Office updater. We rate the threat this infection vector represents as very high, as WPS Office claims to have <a href="https://www.wps.com/about-us/">1.2 billion installations worldwide</a>, and this vulnerability potentially allows a simple way to execute arbitrary code on any of these devices. We have contacted WPS Office about the vulnerability we discovered and it has since been fixed.</p><p>Our research points to some unanswered questions, such as reliable attribution and the attackers’ motivation.</p><h2><strong>Appendix</strong></h2><h3><strong>List of processes:</strong></h3><ul><li data-list-item-id="ea9c97552248a3b986509de138cc5f78a"><code>360sd.exe</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e727937c26b01445317965a5025de15cd"><code>360rp.exe</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e8c6ab30c3fa8d15ffc1cf9d57b6a9150"><code>360Tray.exe</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e06accb1bbd4a3b0063770b2d0df24dfd"><code>360Safe.exe</code></li><li data-list-item-id="ea085d54c3f76cd11d105b1453374727b"><code>360rps.exe</code></li><li data-list-item-id="ed36b429e72d571694d1b72192532492f"><code>ZhuDongFangYu.exe</code></li><li data-list-item-id="ed7b01e271feb93e7df787044d3ae39fa"><code>kxetray.exe</code></li><li data-list-item-id="ee3e9b7136bbaf13a7563bd0d16c3a38e"><code>kxescore.exe</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e6ae90c1ac9d5025dd50785a0b285aec6"><code>KSafeTray.exe</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e8c39d208469af4106ecfcb31dacd7147"><code>KSafe.exe</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e2ea4a70d02e8ff733b7713bc0bbcf3f7"><code>audiodg.exe</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e2459fba6eaab0fe10b28d7b9f19d92e9"><code>iexplore.exe</code></li><li data-list-item-id="eea96af7992811d1789a5b815fb265065"><code>MicrosoftEdge.exe</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e0d1f27d3ecbe8bba5ec4f2da58439ad8"><code>MicrosoftEdgeCP.exe</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e81928e0ac7b69d7a65df518859e338e3"><code>chrome.exe</code></li></ul><h3><strong>Registry values changed by the Zload plugin:</strong></h3></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h3><strong>Core module working directory (WD)</strong></h3><p>Default hard-coded WD names (created either in<code> C:\ProgramData\</code> or in <code>%TEMP%</code>):</p><ul><li data-list-item-id="e9a7796c2a60276555e1a6ea90e411b85"><code>spptools</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e2b09ec8f4cb330d457b14297fa667e4a"><code>NewGame</code></li><li data-list-item-id="e4e06835a4557951f8b23e7912fabcedc"><code>TspSoft</code></li><li data-list-item-id="efc083a48d4b35304d22b8fb88a8e997d"><code>InstallAtomx</code></li></ul><p>File used to test permissions: <code>game_&lt;tick_count&gt;.log</code> – the WD path is written into it and then the file is deleted.</p><p>Hard-coded security descriptor used for WD access: <code>“<em>D:(A;;GA;;;WD)(A;OICIIO;GA;;;WD)</em>”</code>.&nbsp;</p><p>Lockfile name format: <code>“&lt;working_dir&gt;\&lt;victim_username&gt;-&lt;comment_string&gt;.log”</code></p><p><strong>Core module mutexes:</strong></p><p><code>Global\sysmon-windows-%x</code> (%x is a CRC32 of an MD5 hash of the victim’s username)</p><p><code>Global\IntelGameSpeed-%x</code> (%x is a CRC32 of an MD5 hash of the victim’s username</p><p><code>Global\TencentSecuriryAgent-P01-%s </code>(%s is the victim’s username)</p><h2><strong>Indicators of Compromise (IoC)</strong></h2><ul><li Based on this, we suspect that the FFRat codebase is being shared between several Chinese adversary groups. Unfortunately, this is not sufficient for attribution as FFRat itself was never reliably attributed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn this blogpost we will describe the malware used in these attacks and the backdoor planted by the APT group, as well as other malicious files used to gain persistence and access to the infected machines. We will also discuss the two infection vectors we saw being used to deliver the malware: an infected installer and exploitation of a vulnerable legitimate application, the diagram above, we describe the relations between the malicious files. Some of the relations might not be accurate, e.g. we are not entirely sure if the MulCom backdoor is loaded by the \u003ccode\u003eCorePlugin\u003c/code\u003e. However, we strongly believe that it is one of the malicious files used in this campaign.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInfection Vector\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe’ve seen multiple infection vectors used in this campaign. Among others, an attacker sent an email with an infected installer to the support team of one of the targeted companies asking to check for a bug in their software. In this post, we are going to describe another vector we’ve seen: a fake \u003ccode\u003eWPS Office\u003c/code\u003e update package. We suspect an attacker exploited a bug in the WPS updater\u003ccode\u003e wpsupdate.exe\u003c/code\u003e, which is a part of the WPS Office installation package. We have contacted WPS Office team about the vulnerability (\u003ca href=\"https://cve.mitre.org/cgi-bin/cvename.cgi?name=CVE-2022-24934\"\u003e\u003ccode\u003eCVE-2022-24934\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/a\u003e), which we discovered, and it has since been fixed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDuring our investigation we saw suspicious behavior in the WPS updater process. When analyzing the binary we discovered a potential security issue that allows an attacker to use the updater to communicate with a server controlled by the attacker to perform actions on the victim’s system, including downloading and running arbitrary executables. To exploit the vulnerability, a registry key under \u003ccode\u003eHKEY_CURRENT_USER \u003c/code\u003eneeds to be modified, and by doing this an attacker gains persistence on the system and control over the update process. In the case we analyzed, the malicious binary was downloaded from the domain \u003ccode\u003eupdate.wps[.]cn\u003c/code\u003e, which is a domain belonging to \u003ccode\u003eKingsoft\u003c/code\u003e, but the serving IP (\u003ccode\u003e103.140.187.16\u003c/code\u003e) has no relationship to the company, so we assume that it is a fake update server used by the attackers.\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eThe downloaded binary (\u003ccode\u003esetup_CN_2052_11.1.0.8830_PersonalDownload_Triale.exe - B9BEA7D1822D9996E0F04CB5BF5103C48828C5121B82E3EB9860E7C4577E2954\u003c/code\u003e) drops two files for sideloading: a signed \u003ccode\u003eQMSpeedupRocketTrayInjectHelper64.exe - Tencent Technology (a3f3bc958107258b3aa6e9e959377dfa607534cc6a426ee8ae193b463483c341)\u003c/code\u003e and a malicious DLL\u003ccode\u003e QMSpeedupRocketTrayStub64.dll.\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eDropper 1 (QMSpeedupRocketTrayStub64.dll)\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003e76adf4fd93b70c4dece4b536b4fae76793d9aa7d8d6ee1750c1ad1f0ffa75491\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe first stage is a backdoor communicating with a C\u0026amp;C (\u003ccode\u003emirrors.centos.8788912[.]com\u003c/code\u003e). Before contacting the C\u0026amp;C server, the backdoor performs several preparational operations. When asked, the customer echoed the developers' claims, replying that mailbox access persisted even after the victim closed the browser, reset their sessions, and changed their Google password.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAt this point, it is worth emphasizing that claims made by threat actors on underground forums might be far from reality, if not entirely made up, and the same goes for endorsements from their customers. Such claims should therefore always be independently verified. Hudson Rock thus rightly approached these statements with caution, concluding only that Remus most likely obtains reusable authorization from the victim's signed-in browser, while also openly acknowledging that their investigation included no technical analysis of the feature and that they could therefore neither confirm the existence of a Gmail zero-day vulnerability nor determine how the feature actually works.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eRemus' bold claims have since been picked up by a number of security vendors and the media, yet the key questions remain unanswered. To the best of our knowledge, there is still no public technical analysis verifying whether Remus can really do what it promises, let alone explaining how it achieves it. In this blog post, we therefore set out to answer both, tracking down the code behind the feature and dissecting how it works under the hood.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eTechnical Analysis\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBefore diving into the technical details, it is worth noting that, based on the publicly available information, it was not clear where the interception functionality actually resides. The feature may have been implemented directly in the malware running on the victim's machine, but it could just as easily have been handled entirely on Remus' backend, in which case there might have been no code to analyze at all (especially considering that Remus presents the feature as a zero-day vulnerability and its developers refuse to share any technical details about it). That said, the developers behind Remus did admit that the feature works by interacting with the browser's memory, which was already a strong clue that at least some part of it might run directly on the victim's machine.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBesides the email interception feature, Remus has recently introduced several other features, such as a built-in clipper, a fake Ledger, and a reverse proxy. After some additional hunting, however, we eventually managed to track down the code behind the feature and found that, contrary to what the Remus documentation suggests, it is implemented as a module after all.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003eDynamic Configuration Retrieval\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eRather than being embedded directly in the stealer binary, Remus' additional modules are delivered only at runtime as part of the stealer's dynamic configuration, retrieved from the C2 server. Besides the modules, this configuration also defines the stealer's collection rules, covering targeted browsers and their extensions, cryptocurrency wallets, applications, files, and registry values. As these rules can be customized by each operator, the scope of collected data may vary significantly from one operator to another, as well as across individual builds.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eImportantly, this variability also applies to the stealer's modules, which is why the email interception module is only present in a subset of Remus builds. We suspect this is a consequence of Remus' tiered subscription model, with the module being reserved for the higher tiers or provided only to operators who specifically opt for the feature.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAs for the retrieval, the dynamic configuration is not delivered as a single blob, but rather split into several smaller parts, which Remus downloads from the C2 server one at a time. Each of them is encrypted separately using ChaCha20, with the 32-byte key and 8-byte nonce prepended to the ciphertext. Not at all. Based on our research, Remus only reuses a well-known technique documented as early as 2015 by \u003ca href=\"https://kaimi.io/2015/04/google-chrome-and-secure-preferences/\"\u003eKaimi\u003c/a\u003e, who described how Chrome calculates the HMAC values protecting its Secure Preferences file. The technique was later analyzed in greater detail by Pablo Picazo-Sanchez, Gerardo Schneider, and Andrei Sabelfeld in their 2020 paper \u003ca href=\"https://www.cse.chalmers.se/~andrei/cans20.pdf\"\u003e\u003cem\u003eHMAC and \"Secure Preferences\": Revisiting Chromium-based Browsers Security\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/a\u003e, and more recently, in 2025, revisited by Synacktiv in their research \u003ca href=\"https://www.synacktiv.com/en/publications/the-phantom-extension-backdooring-chrome-through-uncharted-pathways\"\u003e\u003cem\u003eThe Phantom Extension: Backdooring chrome through uncharted pathways\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/a\u003e, which additionally covers domain-joined machines and the \u003cstrong\u003eDeveloper Mode\u003c/strong\u003e protection mentioned above. For this reason, we will not elaborate on why Remus modifies these particular JSON entries or how the corresponding MAC values are recomputed, and instead refer curious readers to the aforementioned research.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAs for the extension itself, Remus disguises it as \u003cstrong\u003eGoogle Sheets\u003c/strong\u003e, borrowing the description word for word from the legitimate \u003cstrong\u003eGoogle Docs Offline\u003c/strong\u003e extension. Behind this harmless-looking facade, however, the manifest requests host permissions for all URLs (\u003ccode\u003e\u0026lt;all_urls\u0026gt;\u003c/code\u003e), along with the storage and alarms permissions, and runs its logic in a background service worker (see Figure 9). Every request it makes relies on the victim's valid Gmail session, so once the sessions are revoked (or the victim simply signs out), the interception stops working as well. This is not a flaw, but simply how browser extensions are designed to work: once granted host permissions for a website, an extension can send requests to it with the user's existing cookies, just as the website itself would. After all, any malware capable of silently installing an extension could just as well steal the session cookies directly.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn other words, rather than exploiting any flaw in Gmail, what Remus really abuses is the fact that victims who change their password or revoke all sessions will most likely not notice the malicious extension still running in their browser, which eventually regains access to their new emails as soon as they sign back in.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThat said, the absence of a zero-day does not make the approach any less dangerous in practice. As long as the extension remains installed, Remus can indeed intercept and exfiltrate new emails that arrive in any of the victim's signed-in Gmail accounts.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eIndicators of you need to know\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe risk:\u003c/strong\u003e CheckoutSwitch campaigns use fake parcel notices, prize offers and cloud-storage warnings to request small payments. Gen Threat Labs traced payment requests to unrelated merchants selling different products or services, from Weber barbecue grills to Oral-B toothbrushes. In one case, personal details entered on fake DHL pages also appeared in a retailer's customer account.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe scale:\u003c/strong\u003e Gen observes 1.2 million unique emails from these campaigns daily, reaching 200,000 to 300,000 customers each day within our protected customer base alone.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eOur advice:\u003c/strong\u003e Verify unexpected delivery notices, offers and account warnings through the company's official website or app before entering personal or card details. Even a small fee can expose you to unwanted charges and misuse of your information.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/blockquote\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eOur investigation began with a German-language email promising a Weber Spirit grill. The message claimed to be a promotion from Hagebau, a well-known hardware retailer in Germany, and told the recipient they had already won their new grill.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe offer fit the summer barbecue season and borrowed the credibility of two familiar brands. It also contradicted itself: elsewhere in the same message claiming the recipient had won one, the person was urged to hurry for a chance to win.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIt was the kind of spam offer we see regularly, and our protections were already blocking this type of activity. Recognizing the deception, however, told us little about the business behind the scam. We wanted to understand how these campaigns turned someone's interest in a free grill into money, and what happened to the information collected along the way.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWe began checking how widely the campaign was circulating and following its links. The grill offer connected to infrastructure carrying promotions under other brands, including offers for Oral-B dental kits. As we examined more messages, we found retail prizes, parcel delivery notifications and warnings that cloud accounts or personal files were about to disappear.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSome destinations complicated the picture. An antivirus-expiry warning redirected us to McAfee's official website, raising the possibility that deceptive messages were being used to generate sales for genuine commercial services. Identifying a fake email was only the beginning of understanding that relationship.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWe followed selected campaigns through to checkout and examined the payment attempts they generated. The merchant records allowed us to identify the scam businesses requesting the money and compare what they offered with what the emails had promised.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn a campaign using DHL branding, that trail led us to a retailer's customer account containing the details we had entered during a supposed parcel-delivery process.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eA €9.99 delivery fee, and a shop we had never visited\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe DHL-themed pages showed a parcel containing an Apple iPad Pro. The actual worldwide volume is much higher.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe messages borrowed identities across several industries. Delivery lures used DHL, UPS, DPD and La Poste. Retail promotions and reward offers used names including Hagebau, Lidl, Walmart, Weber and Oral-B. Other messages impersonated healthcare services or used antivirus, streaming and cloud-account warnings. Whatever the brand, the narratives played on a few basic emotions: fear of losing something, such as personal files or a held parcel, and hope of gaining something, such as a prize or payout. These emotions drove both the click and the payment.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFor a separate analysis, we randomly selected about 33,000 emails per day over one month. The resulting dataset contained \u003cstrong\u003e1,090,839 unique emails\u003c/strong\u003e. Our classification showed that the traffic was overwhelmingly abusive: \u003cstrong\u003e59% was deceptive affiliate marketing, 25% was likely scams and 16% was suspicious affiliate marketing\u003c/strong\u003e. Most of the emails we analyzed fell into two categories: deceptive affiliate marketing and likely scams.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn a separate analysis of the same dataset, we found that \u003cstrong\u003e98% of emails contained at least one Google Cloud Storage link\u003c/strong\u003e. These links redirected visitors through tracking and affiliate systems to an offer or checkout. Serving as the entry point to the affiliate network, they may help campaigns bypass spam and other email filters. Gen Security solutions already block this activity, even when the initial link uses a trusted cloud domain.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe language distribution was heavily concentrated in English (51%) and French (35%), which together accounted for most of the analyzed emails. Italian, German, Spanish and Dutch followed at much lower levels. The narratives also varied by language: fake-prize offers dominated several European languages, particularly French and Italian, while English-language emails showed a broader mix of fake prizes and cloud-storage warnings.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThreat prevalence shows a clear geographic concentration in Western and English-speaking markets. The highest prevalence was observed in the United Kingdom, followed by Australia and the Netherlands, with the United States, France, Austria, Belgium, Canada and Switzerland also ranking prominently. The pattern does not indicate strong event-driven or seasonal surges, suggesting that the threat is persistent and significant, rather than concentrating around major holidays or marketing events.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eHow the campaigns use familiar brands\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eLooking at the original email alone would not reveal the merchant eventually requesting payment. Following those routes allowed us to connect familiar-looking promotions with the commercial destinations behind them.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSome brands provide the identity behind the message. A fake DHL notification presents a delivery problem, while a Netflix renewal warning claims that an account needs attention. The recipient is given a reason to act through a service they recognize.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eOther brands make the offer attractive. The grill email that started our investigation used Hagebau as the retailer behind the promotion and Weber as the promised prize. Similar campaigns offered Oral-B dental kits, Makita tools and Apple iPads.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eNews organizations appear in another role. Fake articles using CNN or CBS News branding present health-product promotions as reporting, borrowing the credibility of a familiar news source.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eA single campaign can therefore involve several brands with different purposes. shipping fee. People can lose money on purchases they never intended to make, and some destinations introduce membership or recurring-payment terms. Providing card details through a deceptive process also exposes that information to possible theft or misuse.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe Gearteria account showed a further consequence we could document directly: personal information collected during a fake delivery process appearing in an unrelated customer profile. That reuse creates risks of impersonation and identity fraud. A profile created without someone's knowledge also raises the risk that terms and conditions they have never seen could be accepted on their behalf, including automatic renewal of services and recurring charges. The profile itself was an observed finding. Acceptance of such terms, automatic renewal and subsequent identity theft were not established by our research.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe companies being impersonated can suffer reputational damage and receive complaints about offers or charges they had nothing to do with.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eMerchants receiving the referrals may also face consequences. A business that has not approved these methods could pay affiliate commissions for customers who were deceived about the purchase, then have to handle complaints, refunds and chargebacks.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eConsumers can check the original claim before entering their details. A delivery notification can be verified through the courier's official website or app. A storage warning can be checked by opening the account independently. Unexpected promotions can be verified through the named company's own channels.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eThe business behind the spam\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWhat began with a grill offer led us to campaigns connecting familiar brands with unrelated commercial transactions. The payment records identified the merchants requesting the money, while affiliate tracking showed how referrals were credited. In the DHL case, we could also see where the personal information had gone.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSomeone agreeing to a small delivery fee can end up buying an unrelated product or service. A merchant paying affiliates in good faith may also be misled, funding promotions that deceive the customers it is paying to attract.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBusinesses need to examine the emails and pages generating those referrals and check what customers were promised before they arrived.
