you need to know\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe risk: AI chatbots can help you find products, download software or locate websites, but the links they recommend aren't always safe. Gen Threat Labs found examples of ChatGPT directing users to fake online stores, phishing websites and malicious downloads. Sometimes, even legitimate websites recommended by AI had been compromised by criminals.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe scale: Over 90 days, Gen security products blocked more than 260,000 threat-related events associated with AI chat services, including dangerous links and attacks hosted on AI platforms.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eOur advice:\u003c/strong\u003e Don't assume a website is trustworthy just because an AI chatbot recommended it. Double-check website addresses, be cautious when shopping at unfamiliar stores, and keep your security protection enabled.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eFor years, cybercriminals have manipulated search results to put fraudulent stores, malicious downloads and phishing sites in front of people searching for legitimate services. The practice is generally known as SEO poisoning. As more people use AI assistants to find websites and products, similar risks are beginning to appear in chatbot answers.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePeople are getting used to asking chatbots for recommendations rather than searching for things themselves. And when an assistant gives you a helpful answer with a link to a website, it's easy to trust that recommendation without checking where it leads. Criminals can take advantage of that trust, particularly when they manage to get a fake store or malicious website included in an AI-generated answer.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur investigations uncovered several examples, including ChatGPT recommending a fake EcoFlow store, directing someone looking for legitimate software to a website distributing an infostealer, and linking to a respected journalism organization whose website had been compromised to redirect visitors to scams. Over the 90 days covered by our research, more than 121,000 Gen users were protected from threats associated with AI chat services.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe should also consider what happens as AI assistants start doing more things on our behalf. AI agents can increasingly navigate websites, download software and interact with online services, sometimes without requiring the user to approve every step. The attacks we've investigated mainly involve people clicking links recommended by chatbots, but these same malicious websites could also pose a risk to AI agents. As we give AI more freedom to act for us, checking the safety of the websites it interacts with will become increasingly important.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eFrom SEO poisoning to poisoned AI answers\u0026nbsp;\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eTraditional SEO poisoning attempts to influence where attacker-controlled content appears in search results. Now, a newer discipline called Answer Engine Optimization (AEO) is emerging, focused on making websites and their content more likely to appear in answers and recommendations from AI assistants. As AI agents become more widely used, businesses are also adapting their websites to make it easier for these systems to find, understand and use their content.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;Cybercriminals can exploit these same mechanisms to get malicious websites or manipulated information into AI-generated answers. This is where AI result poisoning comes in. MITRE ATT\u0026amp;CK added AI Result Poisoning as technique \u003ca href=\"https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1608/007/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eT1608.007\u003c/a\u003e in 2026, describing how attackers can manipulate publicly available\u0026nbsp; web content to influence what AI systems retrieve or recommend.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHow do scam links end up in a chatbot's answer? We see four routes. First, the model can learn from poisoned data, so a bad recommendation is baked in before anyone asks. Second, the chatbot searches the web, finds content an attacker planted or a legitimate site the attacker has compromised, and passes it on as a citation or recommendation. Third, the model invents a plausible domain, such as a “.io” address for a well-known app, and a criminal registers it to carry out their scam. Fourth, the AI platform itself is only the host: attackers publish a lure in a shared conversation and send people there. From the outside we often cannot tell the first three apart. For the person who clicks, it makes no difference – all point to fraud.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=iixqL53r 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=FZWxUrVY 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":658},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=JU9PBl4T 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=OMMgcCy4 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":501},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=COHjO9mS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=bhI6P7Rl 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":388}],"caption":"Figure 1. Our current investigation also documents the second route, where harmful or compromised sites appear directly in AI-generated responses.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe 260,000 cases of scams originating from AI recommendations is a big pile, so here are just a few cases we pulled out of it: fake stores, malicious downloads, phishing pages and legitimate websites that had been compromised. Its official domain is upscayl.org, but in one of the cases we analyzed, someone asking ChatGPT for the project website was directed to upscayl[.]io instead. ChatGPT retrieved the original article on the APN website and found nothing unusual, but when the user followed the link, attackers redirected him to a scam. The criminals were using cloaking techniques to show different content depending on who was visiting the website, meaning that even an automated security check performed by the AI platform could have missed the threat if it received the legitimate content.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is why we need an additional layer of protection that checks what actually happens when someone visits a website, rather than relying only on what the AI assistant saw earlier. And as AI agents start browsing websites and performing tasks on our behalf, that independent protection becomes even more important.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCompromised retailers and EtherHiding-based ClickFix attacks\u0026nbsp;\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe saw a similar problem at a legitimate Australian medical-supplies shop. ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-11/Figure_4_BitGPT_Pro_Investment_Funnel_0.png.webp?itok=tddWgMMU","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eWhy an AI crawler and a visitor may see different pages\u0026nbsp;\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCybercriminals have been using cloaking for years to evade detection by search engines and security tools. Their websites can check a visitor's IP address, location, browser or operating system and decide what content to display. An automated crawler might see a perfectly legitimate page, while a regular user visiting the same URL gets redirected to a scam or a website distributing malware.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe same techniques can work against AI assistants. When ChatGPT retrieves information from a website, it may see legitimate content and include a link in its answer. But when someone clicks that link, the website can recognize the visitor and redirect them to a malicious destination. Attackers can also compromise a legitimate website after the AI assistant has accessed it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAI platforms have their own security measures, but attackers are constantly looking for ways around them, just as they have done with search engines for years. Our investigations show why users shouldn't automatically trust a website simply because an AI chatbot recommended it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eHow people can reduce their risk\u0026nbsp;\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen an assistant recommends a software download, check the developer's official domain before installing it. A familiar project name in the address bar is not enough, particularly when a lookalike site is distributing a modified installer. For unfamiliar stores, verify the business independently, including its contact details, policies, reputation and relationship to any brand it claims to represent.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBe cautious with investment sites offering unusually attractive opportunities but little verifiable company information. Treat unexpected login screens, CAPTCHA-like instructions to run commands and AI-shared installation guides with similar skepticism. Keep browser and security protections enabled, including when the link originated from a trusted assistant rather than an email, message or search result.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eHelping people live Fearless in the AI era\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eGen is already protecting people against many of the threats we've described in this investigation. Our web protection helps block malicious websites, phishing and scams, including those reached through AI chatbot recommendations. This is particularly important when dealing with compromised websites or malicious redirects, where the content a user encounters may be different from what the AI assistant saw earlier. We also use AI to help people identify scams, with solutions such as Norton Genie Scam Detector.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAs AI assistants become more capable and start performing tasks on our behalf, we need to extend that protection beyond traditional browsing. This is one of the reasons we developed the \u003ca href=\"https://ai.gendigital.com/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eGen Agent Trust Hub\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e (ATH)\u003c/strong\u003e, which helps assess the safety of AI agents and the tools they use, monitor their activity and intervene when they attempt potentially dangerous actions.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur goal is to help people benefit from AI without exposing themselves to unnecessary risks, whether they're browsing the web themselves or relying on an AI agent to do things for them. Cybercriminals will keep finding new ways to abuse these technologies, and our job is to anticipate those threats and protect people from them.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat's how we help have found mobile subscription services sending payment authorization messages in a format that lets phones and browsers offer the code for autofill. The message explains the charge, including how often it repeats, but someone can accept the suggested code without opening it. In the iPhone example we tested, the suggestion displayed an amount while leaving out the fact that it would be charged every week.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe use the name BillBait for misleading subscription journeys that use SMS autofill to let people enter a payment authorization code without opening the message containing the subscription terms.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAt Gen Threat Labs, we have been examining subscription journeys in Poland, Switzerland, Denmark and Canada. Our investigation looks at the use of these autofill-formatted messages in services charged through a mobile operator, and how that changes the information people encounter when making a purchase.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost of us are used to receiving codes by SMS. We enter them to sign into accounts, confirm transactions and prove that a phone number belongs to us. Having the phone retrieve the code saves us from switching between the message and the website, so accepting the suggestion can become as routine as entering the code ourselves.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor a subscription charged through a mobile operator, however, the message also explains the financial commitment associated with that code. Someone expecting a routine phone number verification may be authorizing a recurring payment.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePaying through a mobile account has been around for years. Premium SMS services have been used to buy content and pay for services. Although the billing arrangements differ, some subscription journeys involve entering a phone number on a website and \u003ca href=\"https://archiwum.uke.gov.pl/aktualnosci/biezace-informacje/komunikaty/wakacje-co-musimy-wiedziec-o-czym-pamietac%2C6049.html\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003econfirming it with a code received by SMS\u003c/a\u003e.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe messages we examined use a format that associates the code with a website. Alongside the text intended for the customer, they include a machine-readable line containing the domain and the code. In the captured Polish message, that pair is:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e@[REDACTED].com #2976\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWith the appropriate website and browser support, this allows the code to be offered for autofill. The domain helps the software determine where the code belongs, while the person can enter it without opening the message and rewriting it across.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eGoogle’s \u003ca href=\"https://web.dev/articles/sms-otp-form\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eguidance on SMS codes\u003c/a\u003e describes banks and card issuers using them as an additional security check during a payment. In the subscription journeys we examined, the code itself is the only authorization and verification to start recurring charges on the user’s mobile account. Someone who believes they are verifying their phone number may therefore be completing a step that authorizes a paid subscription. The SMS explains what the code authorizes, including the price and billing frequency, but autofill allows the person to enter it without opening that message. A code intended to confirm a financial commitment can then be handled as casually as a routine login code.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=zAQsjNnV 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=UFJljuZb 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":889,"height":1109},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=6-fiIQZM 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=GUFD4O9n 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":889,"height":1109},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=PzqhIEg7 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=L10g45RW 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":861}],"caption":"Figure 1. Together, they illustrate two parts of the problem we are examining: how a request is presented to the customer, and how a payment authorization code is offered by the phone.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor anyone encountering these requests, opening the complete SMS before submitting the code is a useful precaution. Check that the service name matches what you intended to buy and that the amount is charged at the frequency you expect. If the message describes a subscription you were not looking for, stop there, even if the website has presented the code as a routine check.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you discover an unfamiliar charge on your mobile bill, contact your operator through its official app, website or customer service number. Ask who is collecting the payment, how to cancel it and how to dispute the charge. Keep the messages and screenshots, and verify the cancellation instructions before following them. Some services use a STOP message, but blocking the sender or marking it as SPAM does not help, the charges will continue.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePeople who do not use premium services can also ask their operator about blocking them, checking separately what controls apply to third-party billing. The available options depend on the operator and country.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOn the iPhone we tested, Apple’s autofill suggestion showed 14.99 zł without saying that the charge would repeat every week. Chrome displayed the full SMS, weekly charge included, but inside a browser prompt that carried Google's authority and was designed to be approved rather than read. Someone who clicked a misleading ad and was asked to verify their phone number could easily treat either prompt as just another verification step, without realizing that the code is actually authorizing a paid subscription to a completely different service.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBoth companies should work with carriers and payment providers to investigate ways of identifying these messages and giving them a clear payment confirmation. Someone agreeing to pay 14.99 zł every week should see the service name and those recurring charges alongside the action they are being asked to confirm. The page was hosted on claude.ai, which is Anthropic’s own domain. It had Claude’s branding and what looked like simple instructions to install the desktop application. The attackers even paid for Google ads to send people there.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnthropic, of course, never published such instructions. The attackers used Claude’s features for sharing conversations and Artifacts, and through them they placed malicious content on the real website. The page did not offer any installer. Instead, it told visitors to copy a command and paste it into the Terminal on their Mac. Everybody who followed these steps installed Atomic macOS Stealer (AMOS) — a malware that is made to steal passwords, browser sessions, cryptocurrency wallets and other sensitive data.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur analysis found one more unpleasant thing. The malware can also replace the legitimate Ledger and Trezor desktop applications with modified versions. Later these fake versions ask the user to type the wallet recovery phrase, and this gives the attackers everything they need to take the victim’s cryptocurrency.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd this wasn't limited to macOS. We identified another campaign using Claude's sharing features and Google Ads to target Windows users. The installation instructions were different, as was the malware being delivered, but the approach was essentially the same.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNeither campaign required compromising Anthropic's infrastructure or registering a convincing imitation of its domain. The attackers simply took advantage of features available to ordinary Claude users, turning legitimate content-sharing functionality into a way to distribute malware.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhen the scam lives on the real domain\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost phishing and scam campaigns depend on small details that the visitor is supposed to not notice — a domain name with a typo, a strange top-level domain, a missing padlock.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis campaign removes all of that. The fake “Download Claude” page is real user-generated content hosted on claude.ai, and it is served in two ways: as a Shared Chat (claude[.]ai/share/…) and as a public Artifact (claude[.]ai/artifact/…). Both of them are normal Claude features for sharing content.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-center\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eMALICIOUS — the fake The command starts a chain of scripts that are downloaded from the attacker’s server, but for the operating system it looks like you typed it.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli class=\"ck-list-marker-bold\" data-list-item-id=\"e659ccd39d70f4d47697b4f8d3d876653\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe command is scrambled on purpose. No legitimate application — not Claude and not anything else — needs you to run a cryptic one-liner to be installed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe theft the stealer can take anything, it needs Full Disk Access. If it does not have this access, it does not start to steal. Instead it shows a fake macOS “Full Disk Access required” message and quietly adds a line to the shell startup file, so the malware starts again the next time you open the Terminal. In this way it simply waits until it gets the access it needs. Once it has the access, the stealer shows a familiar dialog: a fake macOS “System Preferences” password prompt. It looks native and it does not go away until you type a password that really works. After you type it, the malware has your login password in cleartext, and it keeps it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter this it harvests, fast and quietly: Passwords and session cookies from Chromium and Gecko based browsers along with Keychain keys it needs to decrypt saved logins, crypto wallets both browser-extension and desktop applications and also files that usually hold secrets, private or otherwise interesting data such as with .pdf, .docx, .key, .seed or .pem, .wallet, or .ovpn extensions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAll the collected data is packed into a ZIP file and uploaded to the attacker’s server. After the upload the stealer deletes its temporary files, so it leaves less trace behind.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eFake wallet apps\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the stealer sees that Ledger Live, Ledger Wallet, or Trezor Suite is installed, it downloads a trojanized copy of this application from the attacker and puts it over the real one. The modified application looks and works like a normal one.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe trap activates later. The next time when you open your wallet, a convincing screen asks you to “restore” or “validate” your recovery phrase (for Trezor it even shows a fake progress animation: “Reading device info… Validating seed phrase… Almost there”). The words that you type go directly to a second attacker server — a different domain than the one which delivered the malware, and it is built only to collect seed phrases.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThose words are your money. Anybody who has them can empty every wallet that is derived from them, immediately and without any way back.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe same trick comes to Windows\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe also identified a separate campaign targeting Windows users. But the method does not really care about the operating system, and we found a second campaign on claude.ai that works in exactly the same way — only the victim and the final malware are different.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe lure is again a page that lives on the real domain. This time it is an Artifact with the title “Install Claude on Windows”, and it is pushed in the same way: the link carries Google Ads parameters (gclid, gad_campaignid), so the attackers again paid to place it high in the search results. On the Mac the victim is sent to the Terminal. On Windows the page asks you to press Win + R (this opens the Run dialog), then Ctrl + V to paste, and Enter to run. No Terminal, no file to download — only three keys, and the attack starts.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat you paste is a PowerShell command, and it is malicious from the first character — there is no installer in it at all. To look harmless while it runs, it first prints a big “SYSTEM” banner and calm messages like “Challenge in progress, this won’t take long… 50% done… 70% done… Challenge completed.” For the victim it looks like a normal verification step.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat script reaches out to GitHub. The attackers put their next stage on a github.io page, so once more a trusted name carries the malware. The file downloaded looks like an ordinary script, but inside it carries the\u0026nbsp; .NET payload, packed and encrypted.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe final payload\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe final payload is a heavily obfuscated .NET executable.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe behavior however, is clear enough. The malware hides itself by injecting its code into a normal Windows process, so in the task list nothing looks wrong. It checks which antivirus is present, it takes screenshots of the desktop, and it reads the title of the window where the user is working. It also opens a connection to a server of the attacker and keeps it — which means the operator can send more commands later. This is the behavior of a remote-access tool, not of a simple program that steals once and goes away.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd it goes after cryptocurrency. The malware searches the infected computer for crypto wallets — desktop applications like Electrum, Exodus, Ethereum, Bitcoin and Ledger, and also wallet extensions inside the browser.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTwo campaigns, two operating systems, two different pieces of malware — but one and the same front door: a page on claude.ai that looks official, pushed by paid ads, asking the user to run a single command they did not write.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy this goes beyond Claude\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAttackers have been abusing legitimate websites to distribute malware for years. What makes this case interesting is how easily Claude's sharing features could be used to create something that looked like an official software download page, without compromising Anthropic's infrastructure.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eClaude is unlikely to be the last AI service targeted this way. As more platforms allow users to publish and share content, attackers have more opportunities to exploit the reputation of those services. The challenge for platform operators is finding ways to prevent that abuse without making legitimate sharing unnecessarily difficult.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor users, the distinction is worth remembering. A genuine website address tells you where content is hosted, but it doesn't necessarily tell you who created it or whether you should trust it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow to protect yourself\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"edfc88eb7579f843c82adc66243e061d1\"\u003eNever paste into Terminal (or PowerShell) a command that you did not write yourself. This one habit defeats the whole ClickFix family.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec967e55d9b60bb6691b024d2a95e2d15\"\u003eDownload software from the main site of the vendor — and type the address yourself or use a bookmark. The real download page of Claude is claude.com/download. Do not trust the search results, and be extra careful with sponsored ads.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e55f655cf2bb6e06bc90158a0d59c4af7\"\u003eTreat the “user-generated / unverified” labels as a stop sign. If a “download” or “install” page lives on a sharing or artifact URL and not on the own product site of the company, leave it.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e6e92b09b36cd54ff7d342e668284ddba\"\u003eNever type your wallet recovery phrase into software that asks for it unexpectedly. The seed entry happens on the hardware device. A “restore/validate your phrase” prompt inside the app is theft.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"edccf837b687961a074f5261a4b687f87\"\u003eKeep a reputable security software turned on. It blocks the known command-and-control servers and detects this behaviour.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e4e8607ff0369cb902840b9d76a86cd36\"\u003eIf you think that you already ran something like this, expect a full compromise. From a different, clean device, change every important password (email, banking and crypto exchanges first), log out from all sessions, and turn on multi-factor authentication. For crypto, move the funds to a wallet that is created from a completely new recovery phrase, and reinstall the wallet applications only from the official
